Once I have this, I’ll dig up the real underbelly of metal touring life—stuff that rarely makes it into print. **Assistant (2025-10-01 23:45:06 UTC):** # Sleazy Roadie Lore: The Realities of Touring in Metal Bands (1980s–Present) ## Introduction Touring with a metal band is an extreme sport and a way of life.

Expect war stories and urban legends (snorting ants, anyone?), comparisons between **major arena tours vs.

Roadies and band members often speak a dialect outsiders barely understand.

This bookends the day for crew. | | **Laminate** | A laminated backstage pass, often worn on a lanyard or belt.

Usually numbered or personalized – lose it and you pay for a replacement【5†L249-L256】.

A good runner can save the day if you need gaffer tape or a late-night McDonalds run. | | **Bro-Tech** | A “brother hired as a tech.” Nepotism hire who got the job through friendship rather than skill – often found chilling and drinking the band’s beer instead of doing his duties【1†L77-L85】.

Riders can be simple or ridiculously elaborate【5†L331-L339】【5†L363-L372】. (See the *Brown M&M’s* legend below!) | | **Groadie** | Half **groupie**, half **roadie**.

A super-fan who also helps lug gear and is at every show【51†L1-L8】【51†L11-L18】.

E.g. “We’ve got a 5:30 a.m. lobby call?

Each crew member has specific duties to ensure that the only thing the musicians have to do is perform.

The TM is often the first up and last to bed, living on coffee and very little thanks.

They coordinate the **stagehands** (local crew laborers) for loading gear on and off【3†L421-L427】, and cue the show’s start.

The FOH engineer’s ears essentially determine whether the band sounds godly or garbage out front.

They ring out the PA, adjust levels, and battle the acoustics of each venue (from boomy concrete clubs to cavernous arenas).

They change strings (often daily), tune guitars, and have backup instruments ready for every song in the set【13†L13-L21】【13†L49-L57】.

If a string breaks or an amp blows mid-song, the tech’s job is to hand the guitarist a fresh axe in seconds or fix the rig on the fly【13†L61-L69】.

They also manage guitar switches for songs in alternate tunings and ensure all the sexy guitar solos actually cut through the mix.

They set up and tear down the drum kit every day, change drumheads, tune the drums, and mic them up.

A drum kit can have dozens of pieces, so the drum tech’s precision in marking stands and remembering the layout is key.

In the metal world, with double-kicks and elaborate kits, a good drum tech ensures the **blast beats** hit hard every night. - **Lighting Designer (LD) / Lighting Tech** – The person who designs the show’s lighting looks and operates the lighting console during concerts.

Some metal tours even carry **pyrotechnicians** for flame cannons and fireworks – especially in the big outdoor festivals, where “**Pyro** means literal FIRE!!!” as roadies joke【3†L339-L347】【3†L343-L347】.

Safety is paramount: these techs coordinate closely to avoid Spinal Tap-like disasters. - **Merchandiser (Merch)** – Often overlooked but vital for revenue.

On small tours, a friend or partner might do this job, or band members themselves will take turns hawking merch after their set.

They also must settle with the venue if there’s a merch sales cut.

For bus tours, a professional driver pilots the sleeper bus overnight to the next city (often while band and crew sleep in those bunks).

There are strict rules about driver hours – e.g. drivers hit “overdrive” overtime after a certain mileage or hours, and must have 8+ hours off before driving again【3†L307-L315】【3†L309-L317】.

This means sometimes odd departure times (**“Bus call at 2am right after the show, folks!”**) to comply with regulations.

Truck drivers ferry the gear in one or multiple semi-trailers for big tours; they often arrive at venues at dawn to unload.

In club tours, band members themselves often do the driving in vans, taking turns at the wheel on brutal overnight hauls.

These folks handle crowd control for meet-and-greets and keep an eye out for sketchy situations.

In the wild ’80s, some bands infamously had huge biker-looking security teams to keep groupies and paparazzi in check.

Each role comes with long hours and unique stresses – but also a badge of honor.

And sometimes, they even do that – there are instances of crew stepping in when musicians are incapacitated【7†L178-L187】【7†L180-L189】.

You’re trapped in a rolling metal tube (bus or van) with the same people for weeks or months, visiting a new city every day under high-pressure conditions.

Let’s break down some key aspects: the drug and alcohol culture, groupies and relationships, interpersonal conflicts and camaraderie, weird rituals, and survival strategies for staying (relatively) sane. ### Drug Use and Excess “Sex, drugs, and rock ’n’ roll” became a cliché for a reason.

Especially in the 1980s heyday, metal tours were notorious for **rampant drug use and partying**.

Bands like Mötley Crüe wrote the playbook on excess – their backstage was an open bar and open pharmacy.

Cocaine, heroin, Quaaludes, you name it, it was around.

The **Thrash** and **Sleaze** metal scenes alike indulged heavily (one need only read Mötley’s biography *The Dirt* or Slash’s memoir to confirm).

Ozzy didn’t stop there – according to drummer Tommy Lee, that same day Ozzy defecated in a hotel hallway and **“painted the walls with his shit”** in a drug-fueled meltdown【22†L83-L92】【22†L89-L94】.

Such **“chemical courage”** was considered almost normal for rock tours in the ’80s.

But it came at a cost: countless shows were played wasted, some were canceled due to ODs or hospitalizations, and some musicians didn’t survive the lifestyle.

By the 1990s and beyond, there has been a split – many veteran metal musicians got clean after near-death experiences, while younger bands sometimes still flirt with the same demons.

For example, members of Girlschool (an all-female NWOBHM band) touring with Motörhead in the early ’80s noted how Lemmy led them into hard drinking and speed/cocaine use: *“He’s the one who got me into Jack Daniels and Coke… He got me into speed.

He got me into coke,”* recalled Girlschool’s Denise Dufort【20†L269-L277】【20†L270-L274】.

A backstage rider for a metal band typically includes cases of beer and bottles of liquor.

It’s the end-of-day reward and social lubricant.

But it can get out of hand fast.

Others, unfortunately, start at breakfast.

The monotonous routine and nerves can fuel heavy drinking.

It’s telling that many famous metal musicians eventually got sober (Metallica’s James Hetfield, Mötley Crüe’s Nikki Sixx and Vince Neil, Megadeth’s Dave Mustaine, etc.) after the excess nearly killed them or derailed their careers.

In modern times, there’s slightly more awareness.

Bands traveling with Narcan kits for opioid overdoses, or scheduling rehab stints between tours, are sadly realities.

Yet the *mythos* of the hard-partying rocker persists, and some bands feel pressure to live up to it.

Some bands had **“pleasure rooms”** set up, or used roadies as gatekeepers to filter and bring the chosen girls (or guys, in some cases) to the after-show party.

Mötley Crüe infamously described in *The Dirt* how they would compete to find the most *creative* ways to be lewd with groupies.

Def Leppard allegedly kept a “grade book” for groupie performances.

It was a lurid status quo that, frankly, trampled a lot of boundaries and often objectified the groupies severely.

There’s a dark side to that “sex on demand” culture.

Some stories are fun and consensual, but others cross into disturbing territory.

The women of Girlschool, touring as an all-female band in that era, recounted witnessing horrifying treatment of groupies by male bands. *“We saw a lot.

It was awful… It was abuse, of course it was,”* said Kim McAuliffe of Girlschool【20†L300-L307】.

She described one incident where a band **chained a female groupie to a radiator in a hotel room and left her**, only for the woman to show up at the next gig again for more【20†L301-L307】.

The Girlschool members, being women, were appalled and asked the girl why she’d return to such mistreatment. *“Not all blokes were like that, but we did see some stuff that wasn’t great.

In that era, groupies were sometimes very young (there are well-known allegations of underage girls with rock stars).

The power dynamics were grossly skewed.

Many bands now implement rules: *no guests on the bus*, *no means no*, etc., partly to protect themselves from potential #MeToo scenarios and partly out of personal moral growth.

That said, backstage at a metal show you’ll still find local admirers hoping to hang out or hook up with their idols.

But it remains a tricky landscape.

Conversely, there’s the concept of **“crew slut”** (harsh term for someone hooking up with crew members), which shows groupies aren’t only after the band – sometimes the charismatic guitar tech or the bus driver might find a willing companion.

For touring musicians in relationships, groupies represent a real temptation and strain on fidelity.

The trope of “a girl (or boy) in every city” exists.

It takes discipline for committed band members to avoid compromising situations when a star-struck fan is at the dressing room door.

Some bands ban guests altogether to avoid drama; others allow controlled meet-and-greets but keep it professional.

From the **groupie** perspective, heavy metal tours can be a playground or a minefield.

The best of scenarios are fun, consensual flings with rock stars.

Modern touring has slightly cleaned up – e.g., many venues now have strict backstage access lists (laminates and one-time pass stamps) to keep random hangers-on out unless approved.

The term **“Rider Spiders”** has even emerged for *friends or guests who show up and devour all the food and booze on the hospitality rider*【5†L369-L377】 (often groupies or local pals).

Touring parties have learned to watch out for those opportunists.

In black metal and extreme metal scenes, the groupie phenomenon is different.

The vibe is less “party hardy” and more cult-like.

Fans might be more interested in intense conversations about ideology or collecting autographs than casual sex.

But of course, hookups happen there too – just perhaps with more corpse paint involved.

It can provide fun memories or destructive drama.

Small annoyances can fester quickly when you’re running on little sleep and lots of stress.

Even mild-mannered bands eventually have a screaming match about something dumb like *who forgot to fuel the van*.

Money can cause friction, especially on DIY tours – e.g., arguments over how to split a small guarantee or who paid for gas last.

Creative tensions also flare when the grind wears people down; long tours have broken up bands who simply got sick of each other.

Pranks are common to alleviate boredom (more on pranks in the Anecdotes section).

Even conflicts can become jokes later (“remember when the singer threw a sandwich at the drummer’s head in Stuttgart?

The shared adversity – crappy promoters, crazy fans, van breakdowns, border hassles – binds people together.

There can be cliques: band vs crew (in unhealthy situations), or certain band members vs others.

Power dynamics matter – e.g., if a lead singer is on a ego trip, they might treat crew or support bands poorly, leading to resentment.

Conversely, a band that treats crew with respect and camaraderie earns fierce loyalty.

One golden rule that experienced TMs preach is *“be good to the local crew and venue staff”* – showing respect goes a long way【15†L97-L105】.

If a band is rude or acts superior, word spreads and suddenly you’ll find the monitors mysteriously sounding awful or the local hands “busy” when you need help.

Be kind… because they can make your life very difficult if they want to.”*【15†L97-L105】 Reputation travels fast in touring circles. **Camaraderie** is also built through rituals: perhaps the band has a **pre-show huddle** where they all put hands in and shout a catchphrase, including the crew.

Or post-show, everyone does a shot together (if they drink).

It’s common to have late-night heart-to-hearts on the bus lounge, talking about life, fears, etc., in ways you might not with friends at home.

Of course, sometimes **cabin fever** hits a point where a member might bail.

There’s even slang for when someone quits or is fired under murky circumstances: a “**Quit-Fire**” – when it’s ambiguous whether they quit or were sacked【5†L351-L358】.

For many, the **emotional rollercoaster** is intense.

Another night, a technical disaster or a tiny crowd can send everyone into a collective funk, with bickering at an all-time high.

The key is learning when to give each other space.

Some are practical, some are downright bizarre.

Pre-show rituals are common.

Singers do vocal warm-ups (some quite unearthly-sounding), guitarists run scales, drummers stretch.

Gaahl of Gorgoroth might sip red wine in silence for an hour to summon the right headspace; meanwhile, Slayer used to crank up AC/DC and bounce around to get hyped.

Everyone’s got their thing.

In many camps, there’s a last-minute huddle where the band and maybe key crew put their fists in and shout something (as diverse as “**Metal!!!**” to a private in-joke phrase).

Post-show rituals are equally important.

Adrenaline is through the roof after a gig.

Many musicians need a “come-down” routine.

For some it’s meeting fans at the merch table, for others it’s retreating to the bus immediately and sitting quietly. **Towel off, get a beer, high-five the crew** – a common pattern.

Or they might tamper with props – like when Alice in Chains pranked Van Halen by coming out in towels during “Panama,” etc.

It’s juvenile, yes, but when you’re bored in a bus for 10 hours, this is the entertainment available. **Superstitions**: Some performers have lucky charms they bring (a certain bandana or pendant they wear every show).

Hence they prepare extra hard when things seem eerily fine.

Then it’s load-in time (usually late morning or early afternoon).

Crew loads gear, band might do press or stay out of the way. **Soundcheck** happens mid-afternoon: each member tests their gear, FOH and monitors get dialed in.

After soundcheck, there’s often a **meal** (catering or buy-out) and some downtime – maybe an hour to catch a nap or do a quick tourist walk.

After the final encore, sweaty band members come offstage to high-fives and towels.

Then comes **load-out**: The crew tears everything down and packs it back into cases and onto the truck with military efficiency.

In a club, load-out might be done in under 60 minutes; in an arena, it can take 2-3 hours to de-rig the whole production.

Often, local stagehands (**“hands”**) are waiting to help push cases – the *“Ramp Party”* is when they all gather to move the heavy ramp or loading dock gear【5†L359-L367】.

The motto is *“last in, first out”* for packing: the gear that went in last (after soundcheck) comes out first, etc.

There’s a lot of hurry-up-and-wait, and by the end, everyone’s exhausted.

That routine becomes a source of comfort amid chaos.

You grab it when you can.

Naps in the back of the van, “bunk naps” during downtime, even catnaps under the drum riser during an opening band’s set – all fair game.

Earplugs and eye-masks become treasures.

Some carry melatonin to adjust when jumping time zones【44†L139-L147】.

The rule is: if you have free time and you’re tired, *sleep*, because you don’t know if something will interrupt your rest later (early load-in, long drive, etc.).

Indeed, you’ll encounter plenty of late-night pizza and gas station snacks, and alcohol flows freely.

But many touring folks try to balance it out.

You’ll see musicians hitting the hotel gym in the morning, crew members carrying protein bars and vitamins.

A big change over decades is the awareness that if you *only* eat garbage and drink heavily, you will crash and burn fast.

Also, many tours implement **per diems** – a small daily cash allowance for each person to buy meals.

People learn to budget that (you might pocket it and still eat venue-provided catering or cheap fast food to save money).

People bring hobbies: video game consoles on the bus, books and Kindles, Netflix downloads, sketch pads – something to occupy the mind beyond just drinking.

As one article pointed out, doing something normal like exploring a city or visiting a museum on a day off can remind you that the world is bigger than green rooms and stages【15†L79-L87】【15†L85-L93】. - **Respect and Communication:** A big survival factor is treating each other well.

Bands that communicate – airing grievances privately before they explode – last longer.

Crew that feel respected by the band will bend over backwards to solve problems.

Simply saying “thank you” goes a long way.

On the flip side, if a bandmember constantly berates the monitor guy or if the drummer is throwing tantrums daily, morale will tank. **Touring amplifies personalities** – if you’re a jerk, you’ll be a colossal jerk under stress; if you’re easygoing, you’ll be the glue keeping others chill.

Laugh (after it’s fixed).

Spinal Tap moments will happen – like getting lost in a venue’s labyrinth backstage (yes that really happens, as satirized in the movie).

The more you can take things in stride and find humor, the less the stress eats you alive.

On a brutal schedule, not getting seriously sick or injured is crucial.

Tours sometimes carry a **“rock doc”** contact in each city – a doctor willing to discreetly treat band/crew for anything from strep throat to broken bones【3†L381-L387】.

Staying healthy is part luck, part discipline.

Here, we’ve compiled a few infamous anecdotes and legends from metal (and rock) touring history.

After running out of cocaine, Ozzy – extremely wasted – spotted a line of live ants on the ground and proceeded to **snort the entire line of ants through a straw**【22†L62-L70】.

The Crüe watched in astonishment.

Nikki Sixx then attempted to respond by urinating on the ground and planning to lick up his own pee (yes, really) – but Ozzy swooped in first and **licked up Nikki’s urine** before Nikki could, decisively winning the gross-out contest【22†L64-L69】【22†L66-L70】.

As if that weren’t enough, Ozzy then pulled down his pants and defecated in a hotel elevator and **smeared his feces on the walls** for a finale【22†L81-L89】【22†L83-L92】.

The band and crew had to physically intervene.

This was long held up as an example of rock star ridiculousness – a prime diva demand.

However, the truth (as revealed later by singer David Lee Roth) was brilliant: the **brown M&M clause was actually a test**.

They buried the M&M request deep in the technical rider.

If they arrived and found brown M&M’s backstage, it was a red flag that the promoter *had not read the contract carefully*.

So the band would then double-check everything (“do a serious line check of the entire production”)【27†L213-L221】【27†L219-L222】.

In one famous incident, they did find brown M&M’s and proceeded to trash the dressing room as a warning.

In 1992, two of the world’s biggest rock bands, Guns N’ Roses and Metallica, toured together.

During the Montreal show, Metallica’s set was cut short when frontman James Hetfield was severely burned by a pyrotechnics accident.

He was rushed off to hospital, and the crowd was shaken but expecting Guns N’ Roses to save the day with a long set.

Instead, GNR’s Axl Rose claimed throat issues and ended their set early after a few songs.

The already agitated crowd **rioted**, tearing up the stadium.

Touring with Motörhead, they got a front-row seat to **Lemmy Kilmister’s legendary lifestyle** and also the rampant sexism of the time.

Girlschool’s response, as vocalist Kim McAuliffe proudly recounts, was to shout back *“You f**king get ’em out!”*【20†L279-L287】【20†L281-L284】 – shutting up the hecklers.

Another anecdote (mentioned earlier) was seeing a groupie chained to a radiator by another band – which left an impression of how badly some women were treated on tours then【20†L300-L307】.

This was filmed for a DVD.

The spectacle caused an uproar in deeply Catholic Poland.

However, the controversy got Gorgoroth dropped from their label at the time.

Black metal fans still talk about “the sheep head gig” as an example of the genre’s infamous live extremes. - **Iron Maiden’s Plane and Touring Innovations (2008+)** – On a more positive legendary note, Iron Maiden set a modern milestone by converting a Boeing 757 airliner (piloted by their singer Bruce Dickinson, who is a licensed pilot) to carry their entire crew, band, and gear on world tours, dubbing it “Ed Force One.” This allowed Maiden to play far-flung countries by flying everything themselves.

It was a logistics marvel at the time and has become part of metal lore (there’s even a documentary *Iron Maiden: Flight 666* about it).

In one humorous case, Van Halen’s crew exploited David Lee Roth’s hatred of beach balls (he detested them bouncing in the crowd).

It miraculously didn’t break and Scott Ian caught it and continued playing.

These legends are the campfire tales of the touring tribe – some exaggerated over retellings, but many documented well enough to be believed.

They add to the mystique and allure (or horror) of the touring life that outsiders find so fascinating. ## Arena Tours vs.

DIY Underground Not all tours are created equal.

Crowds range from packed sweaty crowds to half-full rooms depending on the city.

More intimate shows, fans right up close. | DIY venues: tiny clubs, bars, basements, community halls, squats.

Crowds can be 50–150 die-hards, or sometimes just the other bands and bartenders.

Very personal atmosphere – no barricades, audience at arm’s length. | | **Crew & Personnel** | Large dedicated crew for every role: multiple guitar techs, drum tech, FOH & monitor engineers, LD, TM, PM, security, caterer, etc.【7†L164-L172】.

Dozens of people on payroll.

Local union stagehands at each arena to assist.

Everyone highly specialized. | Small touring crew, often multi-tasking.

Maybe a TM who doubles as FOH, one tech handling all instruments, and a merch person.

Band members might help load gear.

Fewer hands mean longer setup/tear-down done by band and friends. | Minimal or no crew.

Band members are the roadies.

Occasionally a friend joins as a jack-of-all-trades roadie/driver.

You rely on local opening bands and kind fans to help lug gear.

Fly-in dates use air freight for gear or local rentals.

Band and crew share van bench seats or a rented RV.

Long drives often driven by band or one hired driver.

No frills – napping sitting up, cramped quarters with gear in the back.

If budget allows, occasional cheap motels to shower and sleep. | Personal vehicles (van, SUV, or even compact car for some punk tours).

No bus, no trailer sometimes – gear gets Tetris-packed.

Driving duties shared among band members (who are often exhausted).

They crash on floors or cheap motel 6’s when possible.

Multiple guitar rigs and backup instruments for each musician.

Redundancies everywhere (spare amps, spare cables run).

Fewer backups: maybe one spare guitar, smaller drum kit.

Tech issues are dealt with on the fly due to less redundancy (one amp blows, you pray the venue has a backup or you fix it).

Production value depends on the venue’s capabilities each night. | Very barebones.

Typically using the **house PA** (which could be anything from decent to terrible) and whatever basic lights the bar has (sometimes just a couple par cans or none at all).

The band’s gear is stripped-down to essentials (combo amps, breakable drum kit).

Often no monitors or just one wedge – some DIY shows, the vocalist is literally shouting with no PA support.

It’s raw and gritty by necessity. | | **Amenities & Hospitality** | Considerable hospitality: backstage catering with hot meals (often lunch and dinner) and rider with specific foods, drinks, and comforts.

Dressing rooms for each band member with showers.

Laundry service at some venues or hotels.

Everyone gets a daily **per diem** for personal expenses.

Some clubs feed bands with bar food or pizza, but it’s hit or miss.

One cramped “dressing room” (sometimes just a storage closet or the manager’s office) for all band members to share, or no private room at all – you might be changing in the public restroom.

Showers are rare in club venues; bands often rely on generous hotels (if provided) or planet Fitness memberships for a quick shower.

Per diems are small if they exist.

It’s a bit of a grind, but when a venue treats you well (a home-cooked chili, etc.), you remember it fondly. | Minimal hospitality: many DIY shows have **no rider** at all.

If you’re lucky, the promoter gives you a meal – which could be as humble as a spaghetti potluck or a few drink tickets at the bar.

Bands often subsist on fast food and canned soup.

No dressing rooms – you change in the van or a bathroom with no lock.

No hotels provided – most DIY tours count on **crashing with local friends or fans** to avoid hotel costs. (Couchsurfing and fans offering their floors/couches are a lifesaver【37†L74-L82】.) The level of hospitality can vary: some punk houses will throw a feast for touring bands (the community ethos), others it’s “here’s $50, good luck, you can sleep in the basement if you want.” Expect spartan conditions. | | **Income & Money** | High guarantees (tens or hundreds of thousands per show for big acts), plus significant merch sales.

These tours are profitable businesses.

Promoters are usually Live Nation or major agencies with clear contracts – you won’t worry about getting paid.

Crew are salaried or paid weekly, often well.

However, expenses are also enormous (crew wages, trucking, production) so the operation is complex.

Settlement happens each night with accountants.

Everyone is working but generally in financially stable situation. | Mid-level guarantees (maybe $500–$5,000 a night depending on band draw).

Some nights are door-deals or less-than-ideal pay, balanced by a few good ones.

Merch sales can actually exceed the guarantee on good nights (fans at a 500-cap show might each spend $20-30 on shirts).

The band might break even or make a modest profit if all goes well.

There’s some risk of flaky promoters – e.g., a small club in a random town might claim they didn’t make enough at the door and try to stiff part of the payment.

So the TM’s job includes *hunting down the promoter for the envelope of cash at midnight*.

It’s a hustle. | Very low pay or literally **pay-to-play** in some cases.

DIY bands might get $100 or less for a show, or just a cut of the door after expenses.

Some nights you play for gas money and a meal.

Consistent losses are expected unless you sell a decent amount of merch.

Financial stress is constant – you’re pooling band funds to afford the next drive’s gas or a repair on the van.

Bands often rely on selling merch to survive day-to-day.

Door deals can be handshake agreements, which sometimes vanish if the promoter skipped out – so there are tales of DIY bands essentially stranded due to no pay.

On show night, things run like clockwork: support acts on and off, headliner hits their exact cue with timed pyro.

After the show, it’s a well-oiled teardown.

However, the pressure is high; mistakes are seen by thousands, and there’s less flexibility.

If a massive LED wall fails, you better have a backup or a quick fix.

Everyone has laminate passes with specific access levels.

It’s like a traveling city.

The vibe on big tours can be **more isolated** from fans – artists aren’t walking through the crowd or selling their own merch; they’re in a backstage bubble then whisked to five-star hotels.

Groupies might be vetted by security or restricted.

It’s more corporate in some ways: nightly review of ticket sales, VIP meet-and-greets arranged by management, etc.

But artists also have more support – a sick singer has a doctor on call, a broken guitar has three spares on the truck.

Tours like this also often mean **traveling internationally** with ease: freight companies handle carnets (gear customs paperwork), promoters arrange luxury buses in Europe, etc. **Club Tours:** This is where a lot of metal bands live for a good portion of their career – the clubs and theaters.

It’s a mix of rock ‘n’ roll grit and some professional structure.

The crew is small, so everyone must be a MacGyver.

If the van gets a flat on route, the band might arrive late and have to do a quick “throw and go” set with no soundcheck.

You might even have fans help you load out gear at the end of the night in exchange for a high-five and a slice of pizza.

Financially, club tours can be *just* sustainable if attendance is good and merch moves.

Many bands offset costs by sleeping at cheap hotels 4 to a room, or crashing with friends.

In genres like grindcore, hardcore punk, black metal’s underground – bands often start here.

The conditions can be downright anarchic.

You might play a basement where the “PA” is a jury-rigged stereo and the mic goes into a guitar amp.

Or a living room show where you clear the couches and kids just mosh in someone’s house.

Accommodation is the floor of whoever offered – you unroll sleeping bags among beer cans and cat hair. *Pro tip:* always carry earplugs and an eye mask, because you might be sleeping while a post-show party rages around you.

Driving between DIY gigs, you pray the van holds up.

Money is beyond tight.

You likely make just enough in donations at each show to cover gas to the next.

Many DIY bands print their own shirts or CDs cheaply and sell them for $10 or “pay what you can,” because their fans are as broke as they are.

Bands often swap shows (“You crash with us in Chicago, we’ll crash with you in Detroit”).

The solidarity in these circles is strong – it has to be, because the outside world doesn’t care.

It’s chaotic but also very authentic.

Many metal musicians have experienced *all three* levels over their careers, and they’ll tell you each has its challenges and charms: - Arena tours = high pressure, high reward, more comfort but also more alienation (you sometimes miss that close fan interaction). - Club tours = the sweet spot for many, where you have real audience connection and enough infrastructure to feel like a “proper” show, but still raw and exciting. - DIY tours = the ultimate test of passion.

And the stories that come out (“remember when we played that warehouse squat in Berlin for 20 crust-punks and a dog?”) are priceless.

Most importantly, a writer aiming for realism should note how band behavior and daily life adapt at each level.

New crew often have to adjust to not being claustrophobic.

But the bunk does become your little sanctuary – people decorate them with photos or LED lights, and guard their designated bunk fiercely.

Bus life has pros: you can cover long overnight drives while everyone sleeps (the driver handles it), and you wake up in a new city without having spent 8 hours driving yourself.

It’s like a home on wheels – you can stock the fridge with drinks, play movies on the lounge TV, etc.

There’s even a term **“bunk pirate”** for a band member who holes up in their bunk all day and doesn’t socialize – it happens, especially if someone’s needing alone time.

However, it’s not all luxury.

There’s also the phenomenon of “bus call anxiety” – a lingering fear you might miss the bus departure (hence many sleep with an alarm or get back early from nights out).

If you do miss it, you become that legendary “oil spot” on the pavement and have to find your own way to the next city【3†L298-L304】.

One crew term for someone left behind is *“being Elvis’d”* – because Elvis supposedly sometimes left people if they were late.

The lesson: never be late for bus call.

Buses also mean **shared living**: imagine 10 smelly dudes (and maybe a couple gals) living in a tin can.

Smells accumulate – hence a common courtesy is to **shower whenever possible** outside the bus and use deodorant.

Personal space is minimal: you might get one small cupboard or drawer in the lounge for your snacks and that’s it besides your bunk.

It teaches communal living skills fast.

Lastly, the bus has its own micro-culture: **Bus etiquette** includes things like “keep the front lounge clean,” “don’t hog the PlayStation,” and the golden unspoken rule: if someone’s curtain is closed, that means **do not disturb** – they could be sleeping or having “private time” (hence terms like *“bunk bunny”* sock we mentioned).

There’s also the concept of **“junk bunk”** – one bunk is often used to store everyone’s extra crap (backpacks, etc.) so it’s not in the way. - **Van Life:** A van (often a Ford Econoline or sprinter) with a trailer is common for club tours.

The band and maybe 1-2 crew pile in.

Daytime drives are handled by the team – trading off driving shifts.

It’s physically rougher: you’re sitting upright for hours, legs cramped, trying to catch sleep leaning on the window or on an amp.

At fuel stops, everyone piles out to stretch and hit the restroom (one running joke is how band members can identify each other by the shape of their imprint on the van window after sleeping against it so much).

Vans usually have bench seats that might fold down; some bands jury-rig a sleeping platform in the back if seats are removed, to create a makeshift bunk.

Many DIY bands carry a basic toolkit and maybe know a bit of auto-mechanics out of necessity.

On the plus side, a van can go places a bus can’t – small windy mountain roads, etc.

It’s also cheaper by far (buses can cost >$1,000 a day with driver, whereas a van is just rental or owned and gas).

But with a van, you have to factor in **human fatigue**: after the show, you often drive to a motel or halfway to next city.

Bands sometimes do crazy overnight drives themselves – which can be downright dangerous if someone is nodding off.

It’s not unheard of that an exhausted driver flips the van – which is how some musicians have been badly injured or killed.

So a realistic portrayal must include how critical it is to manage rest and driving duties.

Some bands enforce a rule: *no one drives more than 4 hours without switching*, etc.

Others not so much, and you get white-knuckle stories of the drummer driving 12 hours straight on trucker pills.

Many van tours save money by **couch surfing** – staying with friends or fans.

Entire networks like *“TourSleeper”* or just informal Facebook groups connect bands with hosts【34†L21-L25】【34†L33-L40】.

It’s part of the culture, and great for realism: after a show, the promoter might announce “band needs a floor to crash on, anyone?” and some metalhead will raise their hand.

Next thing the band is at a stranger’s house, drinking beers in their kitchen, then laying sleeping bags on their living room floor amidst random pets and siblings.

It’s equal parts sketchy and heartwarming, depending on luck.

It’s all hands on deck unless you have local stagehands.

Gear is in rolling cases (if you’re pro) or just carried by hand (if DIY).

At an arena, load-in is a huge operation: trucks dock, cases labeled by department roll out, and crew chiefs coordinate where everything goes.

A **“parking map”** might even be provided to show buses and trucks where to go【5†L313-L320】.

At a club, it’s simpler but sometimes chaotic – you might be loading through the front door past the bar patrons if there’s no back alley.

A smart touring crew follows the adage: *“befriend the house tech – they can save your ass or sink your show.”* Many local techs appreciate bands who are respectful and organized.

Show up late and demanding, and you’ll get eye-rolls and maybe subpar effort.

Show up on time and polite, they might go the extra mile to help you fix that crackling amp channel or lend you a drum key. **Soundcheck** is a pivotal moment each day.

Typically, the headliner soundchecks first (they have more gear to dial in), then support bands get a quick line check or short soundcheck if time.

At soundcheck, the FOH engineer will have the band play some tunes to set mix levels, the monitor engineer (or house monitor guy) will ring out monitors and adjust each player’s mix.

This is where problems are caught: *“kick drum isn’t coming through – find the loose mic cable,”* or *“the venue has a strict dB limit of 100dB at FOH”* (some places do, and the engineer must watch levels【3†L298-L304】).

If it’s a tight schedule, sometimes the opener doesn’t get a real soundcheck – they do a **“virtual soundcheck”** (checking instruments individually, line by line) or just wing it during their first song.

Not ideal, but often reality – the headliner always takes priority, and if things run long, the little guys sacrifice their soundcheck.

Monitors are crucial for singers in metal to pitch amidst loud instruments.

These technical aspects are part of daily logistics too – e.g., ensuring spare mic cables, backup vocal mics (a metal singer might smash a mic or throw it to crowd, so you keep spares).

These meetings ensure everyone’s on same page for showtime【3†L397-L405】. **Venue protocols** also vary by country.

In the US, many big venues are **unionized** – meaning you have union stagehands who operate under strict rules (set break times, certain tasks only they can do).

A touring crew might not be allowed to plug something in if it’s a union electrician’s job.

This can frustrate some bands, but you learn to navigate it (or you risk union grievances).

For band and crew, the promoter rep is also the go-to for problems: need more drink tickets?

Need the air-conditioning turned down?

Ask the rep to bug the building manager.

If multiple bands, you often have to **“strike”** the opening band’s gear off to sides or on rolling risers to clear way for the headliner, then do a quick changeover.

If a band has heavy props or pyro, those techs are standing by to ensure safety.

It’s a complex dance but when done well, it appears seamless to the audience. **After the show**, as described, it’s all about pack-up.

Tours develop a **load-out choreography**: everyone has assigned tasks (wrap cables, pack the snake, disassemble drum kit, etc.).

A running joke is that *load-out always takes half the time of load-in* – because now you’re motivated to leave!

Some considerations that are part of the lore: - **Backline Rentals vs.

Carrying Gear:** When touring overseas, many mid-level bands choose not to lug all their amps/drums due to freight costs.

Instead, they send a **tech rider** in advance specifying what gear they need (e.g., “two Marshall JCM800 heads, Ampeg SVT bass rig, Tama drumkit with 22” kick”) and the promoter provides rentals【5†L331-L339】.

This can lead to surprises if the rented gear isn’t as expected (happens often in South America or Asia – you asked for a Marshall stack, you got a no-name amp with “Marshall” stenciled on it).

Adaptability is key; sometimes bandmembers carry their own precious items (like guitar pedals or snare drum) and rent the rest. - **Freight and Carnets:** On larger tours crossing borders with lots of gear, you have something called a **carnet** – an official list of all equipment for customs, to avoid import taxes.

It must be stamped in and out of each country.

If you lose a piece of gear or sell merch without declaring, it can cause customs headaches.

Stories abound of bands stuck at borders at 4am filling out missing paperwork or paying bribes.

But a knowledgeable TM smooths these processes. - **Flying with Gear:** Fly dates require stripping down to essentials that can be checked as baggage.

Guitars in flight cases, minimal drum breakables (cymbals, double-kick pedals) carried, and renting amps at destination.

There’s lore of musicians arguing with airline staff about carrying a guitar on-board (some airlines allow it in overhead, others force it to be checked – risk of damage).

On small tours, the van *is* storage – which unfortunately makes it a target for theft.

It happens far too often, especially in certain “hotspot” cities.

Thus crews adopt preventive lore: *always back the trailer against a wall*, use heavy-duty locks, some even sleep in the van or take shifts watching.

For example, marking all cables and snakes so they get packed consistently.

Some older theaters have limited power – your lighting guy may have to scale back lights or bring in a **“distro”** (power distribution unit) to tie into the building’s electrical【3†L143-L151】.

A band not informed could get the plug pulled mid-set.

So advancing these details is key. - **Local Crew:** As mentioned, large tours rely on local crew for muscle and expertise.

If they don’t show up or are incompetent, the touring crew has a rough day.

If they’re great, things fly.

If a band goes over, it could cost thousands in fines or fees.

Metallica was once fined heavily for breaking curfew at London’s Wembley by continuing past time.

On the flipside, some clubs will let bands play until 2am if they want – it’s variable.

Smaller tours at least use walkie-talkies or cellphones.

A lot of subtle coordination happens that fans never see – e.g., TM messaging the merch person about when to start packing up, or guitar tech telling FOH “guitar amp channel died, I’m switching to backup, heads up.” In the old days it was done by frantic hand signals or running messages, now tech makes it smoother.

All of these logistical pieces are the un-sexy part of touring, but absolutely vital.

The bassist coils cables while still shirtless, the drummer is tearing down his kit while fans try to talk to him.

They might be dead tired but know they have a four-hour drive ahead, so they hustle.

These kinds of moments separate a romanticized view from the real grind.

It’s a life that keeps you on your toes. ## Global Touring: International Differences and Culture Clashes Metal may be a universal language, but touring internationally reveals plenty of cultural differences and unique challenges.

A band from the US hitting Europe, or a Norwegian band heading to South America, quickly learns that **each region has its own touring culture** – from fan behavior to hospitality to infrastructure.

Here we’ll explore key differences in touring North America vs.

Asia (with a special look at Norway and related scenes). ### Europe: Hospitality and Heavy Metal Heartland For many metal bands, **Europe is the promised land**.

The genre has deep roots and broad audiences there, and touring Europe often feels more accommodating than the US.

Several notable differences: - **Catering and Hospitality:** European venues (especially in countries like Germany, Netherlands, France, Scandinavia) are known for treating bands well.

It’s common even at club level to get a hot **home-cooked meal** or a generous spread of cold cuts, cheeses, fruit, and of course, beer.

A touring vocalist might find a kettle with an assortment of tea and honey waiting (Europeans take vocal care seriously).

At metal festivals or bigger shows, the catering can be lavish – multiple choices, all-you-can-eat buffets.

As Fallujah’s vocalist Alex Hofmann notes, coming from lean American tours, you arrive in Europe to find *“endless beer, three to four bottles of booze, endless cheese, cold cuts, water, fruit, veggies”* provided【41†L133-L142】【41†L134-L142】.

Some European promoters also arrange **band apartments or hostel rooms** as part of the deal (so you don’t have to sleep in the van).

In short, Europe invests in band comfort a bit more, seeing it as standard. - **Crowd Behavior:** European metal crowds can differ from American ones in enthusiasm expression.

Nightliner buses are common – you can drive 300-500 km overnight and be in a new country.

Border crossings within the Schengen Area are usually a non-event (no stops).

The UK post-Brexit is a bit more hassle (work permits, carnets stamped).

Driving a big nightliner into city centers can be tricky (some medieval city streets are tiny), but venues accommodate with parking maps【5†L313-L320】.

Fuel and tolls are expensive (e.g., France and Italy have lots of toll highways).

In some cases, trains could be used (Japan encourages bands to use bullet trains, but in Europe, trains aren’t typically for band gear).

One big thing: **language** differences.

While English is the lingua franca of rock touring (most promoters and many fans speak some English, especially in metal scenes), you can’t assume everyone will.

However, as a plus, most European countries have a strong base of rock/metal knowledge among crews and promoters. - **Cultural Norms:** In Europe, especially Northern, punctuality and professionalism are appreciated.

If your set time is 20:00, you start at 20:00, not 20:15.

Also, Europe has government arts support in some places – e.g.

Some Scandinavian tours note how nice it is that **even small towns have great venues with good PAs** thanks to arts funding. - **Merch and Money:** Merchandise can sell well in Europe, but the band must adapt to local pricing (and currency, e.g., pounds vs euros).

One challenge is VAT (value-added tax) – some countries require selling merch with 20% tax included, which you might claim back later.

It’s bureaucratic – many small bands ignore it at their own risk, but bigger tours file VAT paperwork to not lose that chunk.

Alex from Fallujah noted that Europe is generally better for bands financially than the US for equivalent draws – partly due to those provided meals and lodging lowering costs【41†L163-L167】. - **UK vs Mainland:** The UK is a big metal market but touring there is a bit separate.

Gear coming from mainland Europe has to ferry over, and now with Brexit, there’s customs.

UK crowds are typically rowdy (plenty of moshing and singalongs, especially in places like Birmingham or London).

Hospitality in the UK is historically not as praised – UK venues might give decent rider but often not as extravagant as Germany or France (and UK doesn’t exactly have a culinary reputation – cold sandwiches and crisps are a running joke in riders).

Plus, the UK’s internal drives are short, but the cost of fuel and lodging high.

Touring Norway specifically can be odd because major cities are far apart and expensive.

Also, alcohol is expensive – some bands joke that they prefer to stock up elsewhere or hope for a good rider because buying beer in Norway will bankrupt you.

Norwegian crowds for black metal are interesting: black metal grew there, but it’s also not “mainstream popular” domestically beyond the metal community.

You might find more fans of a Norwegian black metal band in Germany than in rural Norway, for instance.

Nonetheless, international fans often flock to see black metal bands in their homeland (Inferno Festival in Oslo at Easter is a gathering of black metal fans from around the world). - **Eastern Europe:** Countries like Poland, Czech Republic, Hungary, the Baltics, etc., have burgeoning metal scenes but less infrastructure in some parts.

Poland and Czech especially have great scenes (Poland gave us Behemoth, Decapitated, etc.).

Clubs there might be a mix of modern and very DIY.

However, budgets might be smaller; a band that gets €1000 in Germany might only get €500 in Slovakia.

Visas and border delays can occur at the edge of Schengen (entering Ukraine, Russia – those are separate logistical adventures, often requiring flights or special paperwork). - **Russia (pre-2022):** Many metal bands did play Moscow, St.

It’s a big market for metal fans.

But it required separate visas and sometimes dealing with shady promoters.

One night you’re in Chicago, next you might have a 8-10 hour drive to the next logical market like Denver (actually much more than 10 from Chicago).

Touring the US means long drives or flights (big tours sometimes do fly dates – but that’s expensive with gear).

The drives can be monotonous (endless interstate highways) or treacherous (winter Rockies or desert heat).

Many clubs provide minimal hospitality (a case of water, some beer, maybe a $50 buyout for dinner).

Rarely will you get the European style meals unless at a higher level or if a local promoter is extra kind.

So US tours often rely on the band fending for themselves for food.

That’s why you hear of bands eating fast food or scrounging gas station snacks – the budget doesn’t allow catering for smaller tours.

Major cities like New York, LA can be spoiled (audiences have seen it all, so sometimes they’re less energetic unless it’s a big deal show).

Secondary markets (say, Columbus, or Austin) might actually go harder out of sheer excitement that bands came.

The camaraderie can be great (singalongs, etc.), but some scenes have issues like over-aggressive dancers (hardcore “kung-fu” moshers).

Compared to Europe, American fans might shout more during songs, not just after.

They also love **chanting the band’s name** for encores.

Crossing the US-Canada border with merch can be dicey (merch taxes, and Canada’s strict if you have DUIs on record, etc.).

However, weather can be brutal (winter tours risk blizzards in Canada/US north). - **Venue Types:** In the US, there’s a whole circuit of House of Blues and similar chain venues – these are professional but often take merch cuts and have lots of rules.

Or they get one motel room for 6 people (sneak extra people in).

Budget constraints are similar to anywhere, but because you can’t easily stay at fans’ houses in some sprawling American cities (no public transport, far suburbs), hotels become necessary.

Some bands get savvy with loyalty programs (stay at the same chain to get a free night later – a trick many crews use).

As one tip from a roadie site: *“Use things like flight miles or hotel points to make traveling easier”*【13†L79-L87】【13†L87-L89】. - **Financials:** The US, despite being wealthy, isn’t always great for band income unless you’re big.

Guarantees can be lower relative to cost of touring (gas, van rental, etc. are pricey).

Also, merch sales can be hindered by high credit card fees or fans who spend more on expensive beer than merch.

In Europe, sometimes everyone (band, crew, promoter) eats together in a common catering.

In the US, you might just have a small green room for the band, crew fends for themselves.

Also, alcohol rules: in some US states, under-21 band members can’t be in the bar except when playing – which has led to absurd situations where band members wait outside until their set and are escorted in.

Or they can’t drink legally if under 21, unlike Europe where 18 (or less) is drinking age.

Mexican metal fans are rabid (in a good way) – very passionate.

But touring there involves dealing with customs for gear, potentially hiring local gear (to avoid bringing everything across border).

And security – unfortunately theft and safety can be issues, so bands often hire local security or use trusted contacts.

But the payoff is amazing shows; Latin fans are often cited by bands as the loudest. ### Latin America: Passion and Mayhem South America (and Central America) deserve special mention because metal bands often speak of those tours with reverence and a bit of fear (in jest).

Countries like Brazil, Argentina, Chile, Colombia, etc., have **extreme metal fan devotion**.

Notable points: - **Fan Passion:** If you’ve seen footage of, say, Iron Maiden playing in São Paulo or Metallica in Mexico City, you’ll notice crowds of 50,000 singing not just lyrics but guitar riffs in unison, jumping so much the earth shakes.

Latin American fans often wait at airports to greet bands (like Beatlesmania for metal).

They might know all the words, even if English isn’t common.

The energy at shows is electric – constant chants (the classic “olé, olé, olé [band name]” football-style chant originated in these regions).

Bruce Dickinson of Iron Maiden once said that playing in South America is like nowhere else; the fans treat you like a hero.

However, that passion can border on chaotic.

There have been riots if shows get canceled (e.g., a Guns N’ Roses show in Argentina in ’92 ended in street riots when Axl no-showed for hours).

For smaller bands, the enthusiasm is still huge – meet-and-greets can turn into mobbing.

Flights are expensive, carnets for gear are needed or you rent regionally.

Promoters usually handle a lot though – if they invite you, they might backline gear or arrange internal flights as part of the deal.

Infrastructure varies: some venues are world-class (big cities like Buenos Aires have good arenas), others in say Peru or smaller markets might be very barebones (PA held together with hope).

Power can be an issue – surges or mismatched voltages can fry gear if not careful.

Also, climate: if you go from the tropics of Brazil to the high altitude of Bogotá, gear and bodies feel it. (Altitude in e.g.

You might get taken to a nice dinner by the promoter’s family one night, then next day have to deal with a less organized situation.

Bands are advised to not wander alone at night in unfamiliar areas, etc.

The crowds also can overwhelm security barriers – there have been incidents of barriers failing and fans rushing forward dangerously.

For smaller bands, it can be tricky because ticket prices relative to local incomes are high, so promoters sometimes can’t pay as much as Europe/US.

Often these tours happen with sponsors or as festival appearances to justify costs.

Some mid-level bands do it out of fan demand rather than profit. - **Brazil specifics:** Brazil is enormous.

Most bands just do São Paulo and maybe Rio or one more.

Fans are extremely vocal (Brazil gave the world the loud “EI PORRA!” chant between songs).

They also have unique crowd traditions: e.g., chanting “ole ole” or just screaming the band’s name nonstop until encore.

Brazilian jiu-jitsu aside, the pits can be wild but oddly friendly – if someone falls, others pick them up (mosh etiquette is universal in metal globally, pretty much). - **Argentina and Chile:** These countries also have legendary crowds.

Iron Maiden’s live album *Flight 666* shows Argentinian fans crying and fainting from excitement.

They also call out funny things – a classic moment is a crowd yelling “SLAAAYERRR!” randomly even if Slayer isn’t playing (just a meme-like tradition in some places). - **Cultural Quirks:** South American time can be more relaxed – shows might start later than advertised, etc.

Japanese concert culture is almost the opposite of Latin America in behavior: fans are extremely polite and regimented.

As Corey Taylor from Slipknot noted, *“fans would go crazy during the songs – and then you could hear a pin drop between songs”* in Japan【53†L145-L153】.

Slipknot members were shocked by this because in the US/Europe there’s always cheering between songs【53†L159-L167】.

Clown from Slipknot learned that *“the Japanese want to make sure the band has the quiet and rest they need between songs”*【53†L161-L168】【53†L163-L167】 – a sign of consideration that blew his mind: *“That’s too much!”* he laughed【53†L161-L168】【53†L163-L167】.

Corey called it *“beautiful – the balance between peace and passion”*【53†L165-L168】.

Japanese crowds often have coordinated movements – for instance, some songs they will all pump their fists in sync or do specific gestures.

They also often don’t mosh wildly (though at metal shows, some headbanging sections, but generally controlled).

If a band invites it, they will, but they err on polite until given permission to go crazy.

After shows, fans line up neatly for orderly meet-and-greets or to give gifts.

Speaking of gifts – Japanese fans love giving bands gifts: local snacks, artwork, etc.

There’s a tradition of it, which can be very heartwarming (but can fill your suitcase quickly!).

Touring logistics in Japan: Typically, promoters bring bands over for short tours or festival appearances.

A local crew (often with limited English but extremely efficient) will handle most setup.

The audience might be smaller in number due to high ticket prices (a club show might be $50 equivalent for fans).

But merch sells well (Japanese fans love collectibles and will pay premium for shirts, etc.).

There are also cultural etiquette: always bow and thank the crowd profusely (they expect humility – anything less can seem rude)【39†L99-L108】【39†L105-L113】.

Bands should be aware of things like removing shoes if invited to someone’s home or certain venues (some places require it; one tip from a touring guide: *“Don’t wear complicated lace-up boots – you may have to take shoes off often”*【39†L129-L137】【39†L130-L137】).

And yes, **comply with strict rules** – for example, some Japanese venues have decibel limits or curfews that are non-negotiable.

Also, Japanese tours often involve promotion like in-store appearances or press conferences – fans will show up to all of it. - **China and SE Asia:** China only recently (in the past 15 years) opened up more to international metal touring.

There are metal scenes in big cities (Beijing, Shanghai, etc.).

Turnouts can vary – metal is still niche but growing.

Logistics in China often require a local fixer – language barrier and bureaucracy means you need a guide/translator at all times.

But Chinese fans that do come are very enthusiastic since they don’t get as many shows.

Southeast Asia (Indonesia, Malaysia, Thailand, Philippines, etc.) has very passionate metalheads, but not as regular tours due to distance/cost.

When bands do go, it’s a huge deal.

For instance, Metallica played in Indonesia and a small riot happened outside by people who couldn’t get tickets.

Many SE Asian metal scenes are DIY – local opening bands are often part of the show and they might lend gear for the foreign band to use.

Hospitality could be great (like they might treat you like royalty within their means) or patchy (promoters working with limited funds).

Expect lots of bureaucratic red tape to do a show, but the fans are extremely hungry for it.

A festival like Bangalore Open Air tries to bring bands, but the infrastructure isn’t as robust as Europe.

Australia’s touring is logistically closer to US (big distances, fly between major cities).

Aussie crowds are rowdy and fun (lots of beer).

Australia has good venues and hospitality somewhat in between US and UK style.

New Zealand is a small market (just a couple cities), but metal fans there are enthusiastic for bands that make the trek. **Cultural adjustments international tours require:** - **Language Barrier:** As mentioned, English is common in northern Europe and among rock communities, but if playing in, say, Latin America or parts of Asia, a band might incorporate some local language – even as simple as “Gracias, amigos!” in Latin countries or “Arigato gozaimasu!” in Japan – which fans love.

Touring crew often learn key phrases or have interpreters. (The Sonicbids Japan guide specifically recommends learning a few phrases like thank you, hello, etc., which goes a long way【39†L99-L108】【39†L109-L117】). - **Tech adaptation:** Different power (voltage) and plug shapes abroad – you bring adapters or you blow up your amp.

Many a naive band has fried gear by not using a transformer.

But metal fans globally understand the horns as metal, so not usually an issue.

In many places, fans or promoters might *host* you in their home for dinner – especially Latin America or Asia, hospitality can be personal.

Bands sometimes need to be mindful of not inciting anything that could cause trouble.

International touring often teaches bands that what works at home might not abroad.

Or crowds might react differently – a silent crowd in Tokyo isn’t bored, they’re being polite, as we saw with Slipknot’s experience【53†L145-L153】【53†L159-L167】.

Bands like Mayhem, Darkthrone, early Emperor played tiny gigs or not at all.

Touring was almost against the “true” black metal ethos for some – it was meant to be cult and inaccessible.

Characteristics of black metal touring: - **Aesthetic and Atmosphere:** Black metal shows are more than concerts; they’re rituals.

The bands wear **corpse paint** (white and black face paint to look like corpses/demons) and often spikes, leather, sometimes armor.

Not typical every show, but some do bring props (pig heads on stakes were used by Mayhem historically – they’d get them from local butchers).

An anecdote: Mayhem in the 2000s would bring a pig’s head, impale it, then toss it into the crowd later.

I’ve heard at one show this created panic with venue management. - **Audience and Ambience:** Black metal crowds often have their own look – many wear corpse paint to shows, or at least lots of black, maybe bullet belts, etc.

Fans might stand with arms crossed, swaying slightly, absorbing the atmosphere.

There’s a sense of reverence.

Only at certain moments (a famous riff or a particular song) do they cheer loudly.

However, this can vary.

At festival settings, black metal sets can have normal mosh responses.

But in club shows, especially in Norway or central Europe, it can feel strangely *calm* in crowd movement – like a collective trance.

One must interpret that correctly: they are into it, just showing it differently. - **Anti-social ethos:** Historically, black metal bands had a reputation for being **stand-offish or even hostile to audiences**.

In the 90s, some would perform with backs turned or minimal interaction, to uphold a grim mystique.

Even now, many don’t do the typical “How are you doing tonight, [city]?!” banter.

Immortal) later became a bit playful (Abbath from Immortal is known to crack tongue-in-cheek “Stay frosty!” jokes referencing their frostbitten image).

But many maintain seriousness. - **Touring Conditions:** Despite image, black metal musicians are human and need the same food, sleep, etc.

But interestingly, some early black metal tours were super DIY.

Dimmu Borgir have done major festivals and support tours (like Ozzfest 2004 in the US), so they adapted to mainstream touring life somewhat, though with their elaborate costumes (they wear armor and robes).

For instance, Emperor’s drummer Faust – convicted murderer – and guitarist Samoth – convicted arsonist – both had trouble getting visas for the US, which affected Emperor’s reunion plans【55†L319-L327】【55†L323-L331】.

Emperor performed in 2006 without Samoth in the US because his visa was delayed due to his criminal past【55†L319-L327】.

Also, in 2014 Emperor couldn’t bring Faust for Japan shows due to his past【55†L350-L356】.

However, nowadays on festival bills you’ll see them all.

Norway has great infrastructure but the cities are far apart with mountains and fjords in between.

Winter driving is treacherous.

Examples could be Arcturus, Ulver (in early days), Dødheimsgard (DHG), etc.

They might stand and appreciate rather than mosh.

It can be heady, almost academic vibe at some shows (people stroking chins at the clever time signatures).

Or if it’s truly avant-garde, like say a band doing a largely ambient set, audiences might even sit on the floor or something. - **Scale:** These bands rarely get huge attendance except in certain hot-spots.

For example, a band like Shining (Norway’s jazz-metal, somewhat avant-garde) might draw 300 in Oslo easily, but in America maybe 50 people.

So tours can be financially tough.

That means conditions could be very DIY – e.g., they might not have a bus, they could be driving themselves around, or piggybacking as support for a bigger act to make it feasible. - **Attitude:** Many avant-garde metal musicians are more liberal/artsy and may lack the extreme misanthropy of orthodox black metal.

So the dynamic is different: it might be less “grim and frostbitten”, more quirky.

Korpiklaani, for example, has a fiddler and accordionist in the band – so they carry those instruments which require care (accordions are heavy and delicate).

Some folk metal acts have lots of extra props – drinking horns, faux swords, etc., to enhance the merry atmosphere. - **Crowd:** Folk metal shows can feel like a big drinking party.

Fans often show up in Viking outfits or kilts (especially at festivals).

There’s dancing – sometimes actual folk dancing in pits (you’ll see circle dances or jig pits instead of slam pits).

Singing along is huge, as many songs have catchy choruses or “Hey!

For instance, Korpiklaani’s “Beer Beer” will have the whole crowd toasting and singing.

The vibe is much more jovial and inclusive – strangers locking arms to swig ale in the crowd.

Groupies in the traditional sense aren’t the focal point; rather, it’s group fun.

Korpiklaani’s rider probably emphasizes certain beers or a bottle of whiskey named in their songs.

And if it’s not provided, fans often gift them local brews.

It’s both blessing and curse: some folk metal musicians have had to moderate to not mess up performances due to constant drinking. (It’s a stereotype: folk metal always drunk.

Or having a mid-set break for a quick “cheers” moment.

They also often partake in the local culture: you’ll see them visiting local historical sites during day (fits their interest in heritage). - **Cultural reception:** Folk metal tends to go over really well in European continent where folk traditions are strong, and in Latin America surprisingly (they love the energy).

In more reserved countries or in the US, sometimes people scratch their heads at a band in furs and facepaint playing polka metal – but usually, once the beer flows, Americans join the fun too.

These shows can convert even non-metalheads because of their party vibe. - **Logistics:** Carrying unusual instruments on flights can be an issue – e.g., a large Finnish kantele (harp-like instrument) or bagpipes (which sometimes get flagged at customs for being odd).

Also, some instruments might need special mics or DI setups, which house engineers might not be used to (“You have an electric hurdy-gurdy?

How do I mic that?!”).

It’s very on-brand and fans love it.

Not heavy infrastructure but more than a standard metal band that just has a banner.

So for black metal, the biggest change is professionalism reluctantly seeping in.

E.g., Mayhem today has proper soundchecks and uses in-ear monitors – something that the early black circle would scoff at as too slick.

Folk metal in early 2000s was niche, now by mid-2010s has robust international circuits (Korpiklaani or Eluveitie can headline tours across continents).

They started in vans, now maybe in buses if co-headlining a big package.

One fun note: As folk and pagan metal matured, some bands matured out of constant drinking.

This is ironic given the genre’s anti-establishment roots.

Also, Norway’s domestic touring is limited by few cities – many bands there focus on going international quickly (Germany, etc., where the market is bigger).

It’s similar for other small-population countries like Finland or Sweden to an extent.

For folk metal in Norway: Not as prominent as in Finland, but there are notable ones (e.g., Trollfest, which infuse a lot of humor – their tours are basically traveling parties).

Trollfest, for instance, has been known to hand out drinking hats to crowd.

Their tours might be messy affairs. ### Touring and Ideology One could mention how black metal bands handle being in certain countries with conflicting ideologies.

Example: Some black metal bands have anti-religious stances that cause friction in religious countries.

Dimmu Borgir had a show in Poland cancelled in 2012 due to religious protests accusing them of Satanism.

Or when Behemoth (Polish blackened death metal) tours, they face occasional protests in ultra-conservative areas or get banned (they were once detained in Russia for visa issues possibly influenced by their “blasphemy”).

Now, on to the final section: ## Evolution of Touring: Then vs Now This ties together a lot of what we’ve said: changes in tech, professionalism, and culture from the 1980s to 2020s.

Major points to mention: - In the 1980s, pre-cellphone, pre-Internet: communication was via pay phones and faxes.

If a show was cancelled, sometimes a band wouldn’t know until arrival.

Today, instant comm via WhatsApp groups between management and crew avoid such issues. - Back then, crew were often hired by label or management with *less* emphasis on safety – hence accidents and wild west approach.

That’s a big change even since the early 2000s.

So, tours are more frequent and often longer – some older metal musicians note they are touring harder in their 50s than in their 30s because it’s the main income now.

This can affect their quality of life (some take families along or schedule breaks). - Fan engagement: Social media means fans expect more interaction.

Some bands embrace it (posting daily behind-the-scenes glimpses), others keep mystery by limiting it.

Bands are more careful since one scandal can blow up online.

Also, groupies posting about encounters can cause issues.

So, many are more discreet or just avoid that scenario to protect reputations/marriages. - Professionalism: Particularly in metal, many formerly wild bands cleaned up.

Metallica stopped drinking heavily by early 2000s (they joke about having “Metalli-cab” to take away drunk crew at parties to avoid problems).

Now, practically every show is recorded by fans on phones.

Mistakes or meltdowns can be online that night.

Bands have to be aware – one off night is immediately public.

Also, touring costs soared (fuel, etc.), so tours often find creative sponsorships – which in 80s metal would be rare (except maybe beer companies).

By 2025, touring resumed but many bands carry rapid tests and have contingency plans for illness (like “if a member gets Covid, we have a backup or we do instrumental sets, etc.” – some did that).

Also insurance now may cover pandemic-related cancellations differently. - Nostalgia factor: Many 80s bands do “farewell tours” now in 2020s, ironically often not the final.

Backstage at those is likely much tamer (families, VIP meet-and-greet events for fans which are very sanitized experiences) instead of the old drugs and groupies circus. - Younger bands: For new metal acts, they often have to be multi-skilled – handle their social media, sometimes even drive their own van, and manage their finances carefully since labels don’t throw tour support money around as in 80s.

Knowledge is more accessible (there are blogs, YouTube channels – e.g., *TankTheTech* is a roadie who explains touring jobs【10†L5-L10】 – that demystify the industry).

But definitely more inclusion than before (like Lzzy Hale from Halestorm always mentions her female guitar tech, etc.). - On a lighter note, *Spinal Tap* moments still occur but people are aware.

Many try to avoid being a laughing stock by planning better – e.g., ensure Stonehenge prop isn’t mistakenly 18 inches instead of 18 feet. **Summing up:** The touring experience has evolved from a lawless, excess-fueled ride in the 1980s to a more polished, technology-enhanced, and businesslike operation today.

Some romance of the wild days is gone – no more open drug use (at least not overt), fewer hotel trashings (bands can’t afford to be banned from hotel chains now).

One can still find sleaze and craziness, especially in younger bands trying to emulate the old legends or in certain scenes (some death metal bands still live it up hard).

Finally, mention saving to canvas – likely just finalizing output. ## Evolution from the 1980s to Today Touring in the metal world has transformed significantly from the 1980s to the 2020s.

Technology was analog (paper maps, no cell phones), so getting lost or encountering Spinal Tap–like mishaps was common.

Backstage culture in that era truly embodied **“sex, drugs and rock ’n’ roll”** – virtually anything went, and very few incidents ever reached the public because there was no social media (a blessing that allowed Mötley Crüe-level debauchery to go on in secret).

Bands had big budgets for pyrotechnics and massive stacks of amps, but also less advanced safety protocols (hence accidents like Metallica’s 1992 Montreal pyro mishap).

Crew members were often comrades who learned on the job, sometimes in over their heads, and tours ran on pure grit and chaos.

By contrast, **modern metal touring is far more professional, technologically advanced, and, frankly, safer**.

This means fewer of the “oops, the promoter didn’t provide X” surprises that plagued old tours【5†L273-L281】.

Backstage culture has toned down significantly in many cases.

This shift is partly personal maturity and partly necessity: with album sales down in the streaming era, **touring is the primary income** for most bands, so it’s run like a business.

There’s greater awareness of consent and conduct; the blatant groupie culture has faded – partly due to changes in social norms and the fact that any incident can be publicly exposed on the internet in minutes.

Lighting rigs feature LED screens and programmable moving lights far beyond the par cans of old.

Even mid-level bands might use **computer-controlled backing tracks** for symphonic layers or effects, synced with click tracks for the drummer – adding polish that used to be available only to the biggest acts.

On the flip side, the margin for error is smaller – if a laptop running backing tracks fails, the show could derail.

Bands often have backup systems on hand for critical tech. **Media and fan engagement:** One of the biggest changes is how bands engage with fans while touring.

In the past, fans might get an autograph by the bus if they were lucky.

Now, bands offer VIP packages where fans can watch soundcheck or hang out (for a price), an important revenue stream that also changes the dynamic – the crew and band have to accommodate fan presence in previously private times.

This gives fans more access, but also means artists are always “on.” Every show is recorded by dozens of phones, so bands feel pressure to be consistently good – any off-night can become a YouTube clip or internet meme instantly.

In response, bands rehearse harder and use tools like in-ear monitors to ensure they hit their marks.

The mystique of rockstars acting outrageously has given way to appreciation for musicians who can deliver night after night.

As a result, many metal shows today are musically tighter and more reliable than some legendary but erratic performances of decades past (compare a sloshed 1986 Guns N’ Roses gig to a 2025 show by a top metal act – the latter will likely start on time and be executed with far fewer hiccups). **Changes in touring infrastructure:** The industry itself has consolidated – big promoters (Live Nation, etc.) run many tours, bringing more standardized practices.

Unions, insurance, and laws play a bigger role: for instance, after high-profile tragedies (like the Great White club fire in 2003), fire codes and safety checks at venues became stricter worldwide.

Tours now must adhere to local safety rules meticulously (e.g., many venues cap crowd size and enforce pyrotechnic permits, preventing the free-for-all of the ’80s club days).

It’s not universal, but it’s growing. **What remains the same?** Importantly, not everything changed.

Young bands in a cramped van in 2025 will bond, bicker, and experience the same thrills and miseries that ’80s bands did, even if they’re using GPS instead of Rand McNally maps.

And metal audiences, whether in 1985 or 2025, still crave intensity and authenticity.

Metal touring has grown up, but it hasn’t grown old – it continues to produce legends and forge lifelong bonds, just with better Wi-Fi and fewer hangovers. --- *In closing, the world of touring metal bands is a rich tapestry of lore and reality.

Whether it’s knowing to avoid the “oil spot” by being on time for bus call, or understanding why the Norwegian black metal singer looks so grim (hint: he’s protecting his mystique while wondering if the promoter has his payment), these insights separate reality from cliché.* Touring in metal is **equal parts endurance test, theatrical production, and traveling circus**.

It takes a special breed to embrace this existence.

Those who do have earned their patches of honor (and probably a few scars).

Crew joke that one wrong move in Coffin Alley and *you’re gone to the underworld*. | **Pleasure Gargoyle** | Viscera’s term for any groupie or fan who is allowed to indulge in the decadent lifestyle with the band.

Often roams between duties and mischief. | **Necro-Rider** | The hospitality and technical rider with grotesque, excessive, and intentionally theatrical requirements.

Must interpret Viscera’s subtle cues: *if the High Priest of Shadows snarls, drop a sub-bass earthquake here*.

Manipulates the monitors so each member feels omnipotent. - **Necrotech (Guitar/Bass Tech)** – Maintains instruments with extreme precision and gore-inspired aesthetic.

Must navigate night roads while Viscera indulges in nocturnal rituals on board.

On smaller tours, sometimes drives during *Oil Ghost hours* to cover up the disappearance of a crew member. --- ## Decadence, Rituals, and Urban Legends ### Decadent Lifestyle is notorious for indulgence in all things grotesque and vulgar.

Drugs, alcohol, and ritualistic substances are commonplace.

Meals may include exotic meats, intentionally disgusting ingredients, or drinks named for horrors of mythology.

The lifestyle is **performative excess**, designed to feed the mythos and shock fans and crew alike.

Failure leads to *haunting* by Viscera’s wrathful persona. - **Nutrition**: Exotic, bizarre, or “repulsive” meals are part of the mythos.
